# CHAPTER 6 SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS IN EXPLOSIVE FACILITY DESIGN 

## INTRODUCTION 

## 6 -1PURPOSE

The purpose of this manual is to present methods of design for protective construction used in facilities for development, testing, production, storage, maintenance,modification, inspection, demilitarization, and disposal of explosive materials.

## 6 -2OBJECTIVE

The primary objectives are to establish design procedures and construction techniques whereby propagation of explosion (from one structure or part of a structure to another)or mass detonation can be prevented and to provide protection for personnel and valuable equipment.



The secondary objectives are to:

(1)Establish the blast load parameters required for design of protective structures.



(2)Provide methods for calculating the dynamic response of structural elements including reinforced concrete, and structural steel.



(3)Establish construction details and procedures necessary to afford the required strength to resist the applied blast loads.

(4)Establish guidelines for siting explosive facilities to obtain maximum cost effectiveness in both the planning and structural arrangements, providing closures, and preventing damage to interior portions of structures because of structural motion, shock, and fragment perforation.



## 6 -3BACKGROUND

For the first 60 years of the 20th century, criteria and methods based upon results of catastrophic events were used for the design of explosive facilities. The criteria and methods did not include a detailed or reliable quantitative basis for assessing the degree of protection afforded by the protective facility. In the late 1960's quantitative procedures were set forth in the first edition of the present manual, "Structures to Resist the Effects of Accidental Explosions." This manual was based on extensive research and development programs which permitted a more reliable approach to current and future design requirements. Since the original publication of this manual, more extensive testing and development programs have taken place. This additional research included work with materials other than reinforced concrete which was the principal construction material referenced in the initial version of the manual.

<!-- batch 31 | page 13 -->

Modern methods for the manufacture and storage of explosive materials, which include many exotic chemicals, fuels, and propellants, require less space for a given quantity of explosive material than was previously needed. Such concentration of explosives increases the possibility of the propagation of accidental explosions (one accidental explosion causing the detonation of other explosive materials). It is evident that a requirement for more accurate design techniques is essential. This manual describes rational design methods to provide the required structural protection.

These design methods account for the close-in effects of a detonation including the high pressures and the nonuniformity of blast loading on protective structures or barriers.These methods also account for intermediate and far-range effects for the design of structures located away from the explosion. The dynamic response of structures,constructed of various materials, or combination of materials, can be calculated, and details are given to provide the strength and ductility required by the design. The design approach is directed primarily toward protective structures subjected to the effects of a high explosive detonation. However, this approach is general, and it is applicable to the design of other explosive environments as well as other explosive materials as mentioned above.



The design techniques set forth in this manual are based upon the results of numerous full- and small-scale structural response and explosive effects tests of various materials conducted in conjunction with the development of this manual and/or related projects.

## 6 -4SCOPE

It is not the intent of this manual to establish safety criteria. Applicable documents should be consulted for this purpose. Response predictions for personnel and equipment are included for information.



In this manual an effort is made to cover the more probable design situations. However,sufficient general information on protective design techniques has been included in order that application of the basic theory can be made to situations other than those which were fully considered.



This manual is applicable to the design of protective structures subjected to the effects associated with high explosive detonations. For these design situations, the manual wil apply for explosive quantities less than 25,000 pounds for close-in effects. However,this manual is also applicable to other situations such as far- or intermediate-range effects. For these latter cases the design procedures were originally developed for explosive quantities in the order of 500,000 pounds which is the maximum quantity of high explosive approved for aboveground storage facilities in the Department of Defense manual, "DoD Ammunition and Explosives Safety Standards,," DOD 6055.09M. \1\ However, DOD 6055.09-M currently addresses explosive quantities of up to 5,000,000 lbs, and the design procedures in this document have been extrapolated for designs to resist accidental explosions on ships of 5,000,000 lbs or more. Although some disasters have involved very high explosive quantities (such as Port Chicago in 1944 which involved around 10,000,000 pounds of explosives), limited testing has been 

<!-- batch 31 | page 14 -->

completed at such high quantities (e.g. the Middle North Series used 1,000,00 Ibs TNT, and the Minor Scale and Misty Picture used the equivalent of 8,000,000 Ibs TNT)./1/ Since tests were primarily directed toward the response of structural steel and reinforced concrete elements to blast overpressures, this manual concentrates on design procedures and techniques for these materials. However, this does not imply that concrete and steel are the only useful materials for protective construction. Tests to establish the response of wood, brick blocks, and plastics, as well as the blast attenuating and mass effects of soil are contemplated. The results of these tests may require, at a later date, the supplementation of these design methods for these and other materials.



Other manuals are available to design protective structures against the effects of high explosive or nuclear detonations. The procedures in these manuals will quite often complement this manual and should be consulted for specific applications.

Computer programs, which are consistent with procedures and techniques contained in the manual, have been approved by the appropriate representative of the US Army, the US Navy, the US Air Force and the Department of Defense Explosives Safety Board (DDESB). These programs are available through the following repositories:

<!-- batch 31 | page 15 -->

## 6 -5FORMAT

This manual is subdivided into six specific chapters dealing with various aspects of design. The titles of these chapters are as follows:

Chapter 1Introduction 

Chapter 2Blast, Fragment, and Shock Loads 

Chapter 3Principles of Dynamic Analysis 

Chapter 4Reinforced Concrete Design 

Chapter 5Structural Steel Design 

Chapter 6Special Considerations in Explosive Facility Design 

When applicable, illustrative examples are included in the Appendices.

Commonly accepted symbols are used as much as possible. However, protective design involves many different scientific and engineering fields, and, therefore, no attempt is made to standardize completely all the symbols used. Each symbol is defined where it is first used, and in the list of symbols at the end of each chapter.

## 6 -6CHAPTER CONTENTS

This chapter contains procedures for the design of blast resistant structures other than above ground, cast-in-place concrete or structural steel structures, as well as the design of other miscellaneous blast resistant components. Included herein is the design of reinforced and non-reinforced masonry walls, precast elements with both prestressed and conventional reinforcement, pre-engineered buildings, suppressive shielding, blast resistant windows, underground structures, earth-covered arch-type magazines, blast valves and shock isolation systems.



\1\

## MASONRY 

## 6 -7

## GENERAL INFORMATION

Masonry walls can provide economical resistance to relatively low external blast overpressures when properly designed and detailed, along with other advantageous characteristics such as limited fragment protection (solid masonry), fire protection,acoustical and thermal insulation, and modular construction. General requirements and limitations on the use of masonry for blast resistant design are discussed in this section.Sections 6-8 and 6-9 discuss specific requirements related to blast design with reinforced masonry and unreinforced masonry, respectively.



The primary application of masonry units in blast resistant design is wall construction Basic variations in wall configurations include the type of masonry unit, such as brick,

<!-- batch 31 | page 16 -->

clay tile or CMU (concrete masonry unit); the manner in which these units are laid (running bond, stack bond, etc.); the amount of void space in the masonry; the number of wythes of masonry units through the wall thickness (i.e. single or double); and, the amount of steel reinforcement (reinforced or unreinforced masonry). In all cases masonry is similar to concrete in that it is relatively strong in compression and much weaker in tension. Therefore, structural masonry walls are commonly reinforced with vertical reinforcing steel bars that are the same as those used in concrete and with secondary steel wire reinforcement (i.e. joint reinforcement) that is placed horizontally in the bed joints between masonry units.



### 6 -7.1Limits on the Use of Masonry Construction

New masonry construction in this UFC is limited to one-story, reinforced CMU walls placed in a running bond with single or multiple wythes. CMU walls may be designed to satisfy Protection Category 1 and Protection Category 2 requirements. All new CMU walls shall be reinforced with vertical steel reinforcing bars that are designed in accordance with Section 6.8. The information in Section 6.9 can be used to determine if existing unreinforced masonry walls provide a sufficient level of protection.

Non-solid masonry walls with any ungrouted voids, including reinforced masonry walls that are not fully grouted, may only be used to provide protection against far-range,externalblast loading(i.e.onlyatchargescaledstandoffsgreaterthan3ft/lb/)where there is no direct primary or secondary fragmentation threat. This requirement effectively limits use of these walls to building sides that do not face towards the potential explosion site. Solid, or fully grouted reinforced masonry walls can be designed to provide required fragment protection as described in Section 6-7.3.7. They may also be used at close-in scaled standoffs (i.e. at charge scaled standoff distances of 3 ft/lb 1/3and less) if they can be designed to satisfy the required Protection Category requirements and spall protection as described in Section 6-7.3.6.

The design of new masonry walls to resist internal blast loads is not recommended as there areverylimited dataforthisapplication. For applications involving existing masonry, the wall may not be subjected to combined flexure and tension under internal or external blast loading. Masonry walls may not be used as substantial dividing walls.Masonry walls are designed to resist blast loads in flexure. Per Section 6-7.4, tension membrane action and compression membrane action may not be considered. For existing unreinforced masonry walls, the additional resistance from axial load arching may be incorporated into the resistance-deflection curve in accordance with Section 69.2.



### 6 -7.2Materials

Masonry materials consist of masonry units and mortar, which is used to connect the units into a structural component. They can also include reinforcing steel and grout,which causes the reinforcing steel to be composite with the masonry, as well as secondary wire reinforcement placed in the horizontal bed joints between courses of 

<!-- batch 31 | page 17 -->

masonry units. Only vertical steel reinforcement that is grouted into the masonry cells should be used as flexural reinforcement for blast resistant design. Wire reinforcement may be used in horizontal bed joints as required for temperature reinforcement, but should not be considered flexural reinforcement. Masonry walls can have multiple wythes of masonry that are connected with ties placed in the mortar beds of each wythe.



#### 6 -7.2.1Masonry, Mortar, and Grout Materials

Figure 6-1 (+) shows typical shapes for different types of masonry units. Brick and CMU are the most commonly used masonry units in the United States (U.S.) and Canada,with some use of clay tile. Generally, a masonry unit is described based on its type and its nominal thickness dimension. The actual dimensions of manufactured masonry units are 3/8 inch less than nominal dimensions to allow for the mortar thickness and the thickness of typical interior wall material (i.e. gypsum board). All masonry units must conform to applicable ASTM requirements in TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5 (e.g. CMU must conform to the requirements of ASTM C90). As shown in Figure 6-1, there are many variations of CMU block shapes, including the conventional "stretcher" block,bond beam units, and "A" and "H" shaped blocks that are placed around vertical reinforcement rather than lifted over the reinforcement. All commonly used CMU configurations that comply with ASTM C90 are acceptable for blast design.

Mortar should conform to the requirements of ASTM 270. There are several types of mortar (i.e. Type M, Type S, and Type N) that are commonly used to atach the masonry units together. There are no available data indicating that any of these mortar types are more effective against blast loads. Therefore, all mortar types are acceptable for blast resistant walls. Grout, which is very similar to concrete without large aggregate,is used in the cells of CMU and in the void space between brick and CMU wythes to bond reinforcing bars to the masonry. The grout should comply with the requirements of ASTM 476 and have sufficient plasticity and vibration during placement to avoid air voids or honeycombing unless self-consolidating grout is used. Construction of blast resistant masonry walls should comply with Level C Quality Assurance according to Chapter 1 of TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5. Although only the reinforced cells in CMU are typically grouted in conventional design, all cells are commonly grouted for explosive safety design.



The manufacturing of CMU depends in part on locally available materials, but most modern CMU construction is with lightweight CMU. Heavier weight CMU has been used more in older construction and cold weather climates. lt is usually conservative to assume lightweight CMU(ie.a unit weight of approximately 95 Ib/ft) for blast design or analysis because this will result in the least calculated mass and corresponding inertial resisting force.



Table 6-1 (+) shows cross sectional property information for typical CMU based on minimum cross section dimensions from the National Concrete Masonry Association (2007). The section modulus, which is not shown in the table, is equal to the moment of 

<!-- batch 31 | page 18 -->

inertia divided by one-half the masonry thickness. The properties of grouted CMU walls can be calculated as shown in Equation 6-1.



#### 6 -7.2.2Steel Reinforcement

Primary vertical reinforcing steel for masonry walls should be deformed steel bar conforming to ASTM A615 or A706, which is the same as concrete reinforcement.Typically, CMU walls also have joint reinforcement conforming to the requirements of ASTM A951 placed in the horizontal joints to control shrinkage. This wire is typically carbon steel wire that is hot dip galvanized after fabrication for use in exterior walls, and is available in two standard sizes, W1.7 (0.148 inch diameter) and W2.8 (0.188 inch diameter). For blast design, joint reinforcement functions as secondary steel reinforcement that helps to distribute blast loads into the vertical reinforcement.

Figure 6-2 (+) shows masonry walls with vertical reinforcing steel (primary 

reinforcement) and horizontal reinforcing steel and wire reinforcement placed in the joints (secondary reinforcement). Horizontal ladder joint reinforcement, as shown Figure 6-2, is recommended for reinforced masonry CMU walls rather than truss type joint reinforcement because it interferes much less with grout placement in cells. Horizontal reinforcing steel can also be placed in bond beams that are constructed with special masonry blocks with notched webs, as shown in Figure 6-2. The reinforcement in bond beams may not be used as primary flexural reinforcing steel to resist out-of-plane blast loads in masonry construction.



Openings in masonry walls should be limited as much as possible, both in size and in number. Supplementary vertical reinforcing should be provided on each side of an opening to transfer the blast load over the tributary supported area of the blast resistant door, window, or plate over the opening to the wall's supports. If possible, the windows,plates, or doors covering openings should be designed to span horizontally to the vertical supports along each side of the opening. However, bond beams and lintels above and below an opening may be designed to resist blast load from the tributary supported area of the door or panel over a span length between the vertical reinforcement on each side of the opening.



### 6 -7.3Material Strengths

Material strengths, including strengths for masonry response in shear, spall, and fragment penetration, are discussed in this section. Dynamic increase factors can be applied to the material strengths of masonry and reinforcing steel in accordance with Section 6-7.3.8Other than this,dynamic increase factors should not be used for blast design for masonry.



#### 6 -7.3.1Reinforcing Steel Strength

Steel reinforcing bars in new reinforced masonry walls should conform to Grades 60 or 75 of ASTM A615 or A706 steel. Existing steel reinforcement may have a lower yield strength of 40 ksi. The modulus of elasticity of the reinforcement is equal to 29,000,000psi.



<!-- batch 31 | page 20 -->

#### 6 -7.3.2Masonry Compression Strength

The compression strength of the masonry units, grout, and mortar acting as a unit are measured together in a masonry prism, which consists of two masonry units with a mortared joint that is tested in compression. The cells of the prism are grouted if the wall is grouted. If the wall construction is partially grouted (contains both ungrouted and grouted cells), then grouted and ungrouted prisms are tested and the compression strength is averaged. The prism is constructed in the field using the materials and workmanship at the jobsite and then tested in the laboratory in a manner similar to concrete cylinders. The resulting compression strength $(\pmb{f}_{m}^{r})$ is used in design similarly to the concrete cylinder strength $(\pmb{f}_{c}^{r})$ . For new blast resistant design, a minimum strength of 2000 psi is required for $\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{m}}$ . Additionally, a minimum grout strength of 300 psi is required to enhance composite action between the reinforcing steel and masonry. The modulus of elasticity of a masonry component can be calculated as shown in Equation 6-2. Equation 6-2 is taken from TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5.



$$E_{m}=K_{m}f_{m}^{\prime}$$

where:

$$\begin{array}{r l}{E_{m}=}&{{}m o d u l u s\;o f e l a s t i c i t y}\\ {f_{m}^{\prime}=}&{{}m a s o n r y\;p r i s m\;s t r e n g t h r}\\ {K_{m}=}&{{}900\;f o r\;C M U}\\ {=}&{{}700\;f o r\;c l a y\;t i l e}\end{array}$$

#### 6 -7.3.3Masonry Flexural Design

The flexural strength of masonry for blast design is based on a dynamic flexural tensile strength (i.e. modulus of rupture), fd, of 200 psi.$\mathsf{I f f}_{\mathsf{m}}$ exceeds 2000 psi, fdt can be taken as 10% off'm provided that it does not exceed 250 psi. These values represent the dynamic adhesion strength between the mortar and masonry along a bed joint at strainrates representative of masonry response to far-range blast loading. Masonry flexural strength is only used in the analysis of unreinforced masonry walls since the flexural strength of reinforced masonry walls is based on the tensile strength of the reinforcing steel, as discussed in Section 6-8.



The values for $\mathsf{f_{d t}}$ were developed by a trial and error procedure where they caused single-degree-of-freedom (SDOF) analyses to match the measured response of unreinforced masonry walls well, on the average, from a large number of high explosive tests (i.e. over 50 tests), where wall response was observed primarily in terms of damage levels and a limited number of measured deflections (Oswald, 2005). These values for $\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{d t}}$ are applicable for modern masonry construction (i.e. since 1960) that is in good condition, laid in running-bond pattern, and without substantial infilling of former 

<!-- batch 31 | page 21 -->

openings.Masonry walls in poor condition (e.g. deteriorating mortar) should not be assumed to have any significant blast resistance.



#### 6 -7.3.4Shear (Diagonal Tension) Strength

Masonry walls develop maximum out-of-plane shear stress near their supports due to flexural response to blast load. Also, the support shear or reaction load applied to the roof diaphragm by the response of walls to blast load on the perpendicular side of the building causes in-plane shear stress on masonry walls that are shear walls in the lateral load resisting system for the building. Both types of shear stress are related to the ultimate support shear,$\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{u}}$ , as described in Section 3-15. The in-plane and out-ofplane shear strengths are calculated as shown in Equation 6-3. The masonry cross sectional areas resisting out-of-plane and in-plane shear (i.e. net areas) are calculated with Equation 6-4a and Equation 6-4b, respectively. The net shear area of fully grouted CMU or solid masonry walls for both in-plane and out-of-plane shear is equal to the ful cross sectional area. Only in-plane shear caused by the reaction loads from blastloaded walls must be considered. The masonry shear strength of walls with net tension load over the cross section is zero.



The net shear area for out-of-plane shear loading of partially grouted CMU or other nonsolid masonry is the solid area of the masonry plus the area of any grouted voids. The critical section for the maximum out-of-plane shear force near the support is determined in the same manner as for concrete (see Figure 4-14 (+) and Section 4-27).The net area for in-plane shear loading of load bearing CMU or other non-solid masonry is the face shell area plus the area of any grouted cores. The net shear areas are defined in Equation 6-4a and Equation 6-4b. Equation 6-3 is generally based on TMS 402/ACI 530/AScE 5, conservatively without consideration of compressive axial load, and blast test data where heavily reinforced CMU walls failed in shear.



$$V_{m}=2\sqrt{f^{'}{}_{m}}A_{n}$$

where:

$V_{m}=$ dynamic shear strength of masonry (Ib/in)

$f_{m}=$ masonry prism compressive strength (psi)

$A_{n}=$ net cross section for shear (in2/in)

二$A_{n o}$ from Equation 6-4a for out-of-plane shear loading $A_{n i}$ from Equation 6-4b for in-plane shear loading 

$$A_{n o}=\left(A_{r s}+\left(1-A_{r s}\right)\frac{V}{100}\right)d $$

masonry.

$A_{f s}=$ solid area ratio of masonry cross sectional area (see Table 6-1 (+))

$V=$ percentage of void area that is grouted 



$$A_{n i}=\left(K_{v}T+(1-K_{v})2t_{s}\right)\frac{L_{v}}{L}$$

In cases where the ultimate support shear force at the critical shear section exceeds the masonry shear strength,$\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{m}},$ , in Equation 6-3, shear reinforcement with an area Av should be designed to carry the excess shear force. The summed shear resistance from the masonry and shear reinforcement for in-plane and out-of-plane shear (considered separately) should not exceed Equation 6-6. Both Equation 6-5 and Equation 6-6 are based on TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5. For out-of-plane shear, the shear reinforcement in Equation 6-5 should be spaced at a maximum distance of d/2,where d is defined in Equation 6-5, to ensure that the reinforcement will cross the shear failure plane. This is typically a spacing of one-half the wall thickness. Unfortunately,this requirement causes the use of stirrups (as used in concrete construction) to be impractical for single wythe clay tile and CMU walls because the stirrups can only be placed in the bed joints (i.e. at 8 inch spacing) and these types of masonry are usually only manufactured up to 12 inches thick, although 14 inch and 16 inch units are available in some areas. Therefore, masonry walls usually must be designed so that the out-of-plane masonry shear strength exceeds the design shear force.

$$A_{v}=\frac{V_{s}s_{v}}{0.5f_{d v}d}$$

#### 6 -7.3.5Direct Shear Strength

Direct shear stresses can develop in components due to early time response that is dominated by a shear response mode, rather than flexure. After this very early time response, flexural response dominates and causes diagonal shear stresses.Thedirect 

<!-- batch 31 | page 24 -->

shear strength of grout in the cells of reinforced masonry can be calculated in the same manner as concrete (see Section 4-19.2), using 16%he grout compression strength.Calculations considering variables causing worse case direct shear stresses indicate that no direct shear failure is expected for reinforced masonry walls that satisfy all of the following requirements: 1) the wall may be exposed to external blast load only, 2) the chareall between supports) is 8ft or greater. A large database of blast tests on unreinforced masonry walls with far-range blast loading also indicates that direct shear is not a failure mode of concern for these walls, which are very weak in flexure. Therefore, direct shear in the masonry wall itself is not typically a design concern for masonry walls subject to far-range loading.



Walls must always be designed to resist direct shear at the supports if the wall is subject to close-in blast loading or if there are any joints at the supports.Thisincludes the typical case where the wall is placed on top of a concrete slab that provides lateral support. Shear friction may be used to resist direct shear forces across a joint in the wall if the reinforcing bars are developed across the joint. For example, dowel reinforcing bars may be considered as shear friction reinforcement if they are developed across the joint between the foundation and the wall. This is also true for flexural bars,since direct shear is assumed to occur before significant flexural response. The direct shear strength in shear friction,$\mathsf{V}_{\mathtt{d m}}$ , can be calculated as shown in Equation 6-8. The development of reinforcing steel in masonry is addressed in Section 6-8.

$$V_{d m}=0.6A_{v,d}f_{d v}$$

where:

$V_{d m}=$ dynamic shear friction strength per unit width along wall (Ib/in)$A_{v d}=a r e a$ I of reinforcing steel that is developed on both sides of direct shear plane between the masonry wall and foundation per unit width along wall (in/in)$\pmb{f}_{d y}=$ dynamic yield strength of reinforcing across direct shear plane (psi)

#### 6 -7.3.6Direct Spalling and Breach Resistance

Spalling and breach of solid or fully grouted masonry walls can be predicted using the same approach shown for concrete in Section 4-55, with the exception that the masonry wall is required to have a thickness that is 1.5 times greater than that required for concrete in Equation 4-178. Also, the masonry prism compression strength,$\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{m}},$ should be used in place of the concrete compression strength,$\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{c}},$ in Equation 4-180a.The safety factor of 1.5, coupled with the overall requirement of the 1.2safety factor on the charge weight in Chapter 1, is required until there is adequate test data for spalling and breach of solid masonry walls to determine a more accurate approach.

<!-- batch 31 | page 25 -->

Additionally, CMU masonry with any ungrouted cells should not be used when the peak design blast pressure exceeds 40 psi.Tests have shown that the face shells of ungrouted masonry cells can fail as short beams spanning between webs during very early time response to these peak blast pressures, thus preventing ungrouted cells from spanning between reinforced cells in partially reinforced CMU walls.

#### 6 -7.3.7Penetration Resistance

Only solid masonry walls should be used for walls subject to fragment impact from an accidental explosion. Fragment penetration through solid masonry should be determined with the same approach as penetration through concrete in Section 4-60.4using the masonry material compression strength,$\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{m o}}$ ,in place of the concrete compression strength,$\mathbf{f}_{\circ}$ .The value of $\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{m o}}$ should be the lesser of the masonry block or grout compression strength.Conservatively,$\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{m o}}$ :can be assumed equal to 1900 psi for masonry that is in good condition unless more accurate information is available.Calculations using the procedures outlined in this section (i.e. using concrete penetration equations in Section 4-60 and values for $\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{m o}}$ in place of $\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{c}})$ overpredicted all measured penetration depths and matched all perforation cases for experiments where twenty-five small steel cubes (i.e. 0.5 inch and 0.63 inch) were shot into unreinforced CMU, grouted CMU, and brick walls at velocities between 3000 ft/sec and 5000 ft/sec (Bowles, et al 1998).



#### 6 -7.3.8Dynamic Increase Factors

There is very limited test data on the relationship between strain-rate and masonry properties. Dynamic increase factors (DIF) and static strength increase factors (SiF)similar to those for reinforced concrete with far-range loading (see Chapter 4) are considered applicable for reinforced masonry.. The dynamic design strengths for reinforced masonry based on these DIF and SIF are shown in Table 6-2 (+)Conservatively, these SIF and DIF can also be used for close-in range loading Otherwise, no DIF or SIF should be used for masonry design.

###### 6 -7.4Masonry Wall Construction.6-7.4.1Multi-Wythe Walls

A masonry wall can be constructed as a single, monolithic wythe with one or more units through the thickness acting compositely, or as a cavity wall consisting of multiple,closely spaced masonry wythes with an air gap or insulation between wythes. Many modern masonry walls in the northern part of the U.S. and Canada are cavity walls to provide required insulation and help prevent moisture migration into the building. Figure 6-3 (+) shows a cavity wall constructed with brick and CMU. Cavity walls can also be constructed with other types of masonry. These walls are connected together across the gap between wythes with ties or tabs, as illustrated in Figure 6-3, which cause both wythes to deflect together in a non-composite manner when conventional lateral loads are applied to the outer wythe. The outer wythe of multi-wythe walls may be referred to as a veneer wall, which is typically an unreinforced, non-structural wall that acts only as a facade and prevents moisture penetration.



New blast-resistant multi-wythe masonry walls should be designed assuming only the inner wythe acts as a structural wythe. Therefore, the inner wythe requires reinforcement, and the outer wythe may be unreinforced and acts only as additional mass. The ties connecting the wythes can be designed for conventional loads for this case, assuming the ties may fail during blast loading. However, failure of the outer wythe is a concern when the failed debris can fall onto an inhabited area or low roof area below. For cases where multi-wythe walls must stay attached, the ties should be designed in tension and compression for a pressure load equal to the smaller of; 1) the ultimate resistance of the outer wythe in flexure, 2) the ultimate resistance of the inner wythe in flexure, or 3) twice the peak blast pressure, acting over the tributary area supported by the tie with a safety factor of 1.3. The effects of any eccentricity in the 

<!-- batch 31 | page 27 -->

connection points of the ties into each wythe on the connector capacity should be considered.



In conventional construction, multi-wythe walls with the same material in each wythe can be designed to act compositely, where the inner wall is in tension and the outer wal is in compression. However, this approach is impractical and is not recommended for blast design of reinforced masonry walls because of the large number of steel stirrups required between the wythes to transfer the full dynamic yield strength of the reinforcing steel into the adjacent wythe. Composite and non-composite unreinforced multi-wythe masonry walls are discussed more in Section 6.9.3.



#### 6 -7.4.2Connections

Blast resistant masonry walls must have a connection at each support consisting of a connection plate bearing against the supporting component, embedded steel reinforcement (i.e. steel dowels into the foundation), or other form of positive connection that will resist the design reaction load from inbound and rebound response of the wall as determined by dynamic analyses (see Section 6-7.6). Usually a bearing connection is only effective for response in one direction (i.e. inbound wall response). Figure 6-4a (+) and Figure 6-4b (+) show typical connections between non-load bearing masonry walls and supporting frame members in conventional construction. These connections are not recommended. They have relatively low strengths and typically, will not be sufficient to resist the design reaction loads. Connections for blast-loaded walls are often steel angles or plates that are bolted into the wall and supporting frame or foundation or welded to embedded structural steel plates. Figure 6-5 (+) shows generic blast-resistant connections.



#### 6 -7.4.3Foundation Considerations

The foundation system, such as that shown in Figure 6-6 (+), provides support at the bottom of masonry walls. Typically, the bottom of a masonry wall is connected with reinforcing steel dowels to a foundation consisting of a continuous reinforced concrete or CMU stem wall and spread footing. Conservatively, this connection can be assumed as a pinned connection when analyzing the flexural response of the wall, which limits the wall resistance to blast load, the reaction force at the foundation, and the corresponding shear stress in the wall. To help ensure that the wall responds consistently with this assumption, the splice between the dowels and vertical reinforcing steel should be limited to the compression splice length as would be typical for conventional design. Alternatively, for CMU walls designed to withstand an external blast load only, a full tension splice length located out of the maximum moment region can be provided and the wall's support at the foundation may be analyzed as fixed if it can be shown that the ultimate soil pressures acting on the foundation can provide a resisting moment equal the ultimate dynamic moment capacity of the wall cross section at the base of the wall 



Figure 6-7 (+) and Figure 6-8 (+) ilustrate the calculation of the resisting moment provided by the foundation at the bottom of a reinforced masonry wall using soil 

<!-- batch 31 | page 28 -->

parameters intended to represent a medium strength soil and a typical range of foundation dimensions. These calculated soil resisting moments can be compared for reference to the ultimate dynamic moment capacity of an 8 inch CMU wall reinforced at midthickness with a 5/8 inch reinforcing steel, which is 11,400 Ib-in/in. This comparison indicates that cohesive soils are more likely to provide the necessary moment resistance to cause fixity at the bottom of a reinforced masonry wall than granular soil.

The resisting moment from cohesive soil in Figure 6-7 (+) is calculated using Brom's method to determine lateral soil pressure. The resisting moment from granular soil in Figure 6-8 (+) is calculated using Rankine's method to determine lateral soil pressures.Other design-based methodologies commonly used for lateral design of foundation systems or finite-element methods validated against data may also be used.

#### 6 -7.4.4Framing and Shear Wall Considerations

Masonry walls can function in the overall building system as load-bearing components or as non-load bearing components. In the latter case, the walls can be continuous in front of the supporting frame members or infill walls placed in the same plane as the frame between supporting frame members. The supporting frame can be a flexible type framing, such as structural steel framing, or more rigid framing, such as reinforced concrete frames. Figure 6-9 (+) illustrates these masonry support systems. Frame rigidity is important for infill masonry walls since it influences the amount of compression membrane response that can occur in masonry walls, as discussed in Section 6-7.5.

Masonry walls can also act within a building system as shear walls. If the shear wall is exterior, it will nearly simultaneously resist directly applied blast loads and shear loads from the roof and floor diaphragms. In this case, the wall should have sufficient shear capacity to resist the combined out-of-plane shear force and in-plane shear force from the floor and roof diaphragms caused by blast loading as discussed in Section 6-7.3.4.

### 6 -7.5Compression and Tension Membrane Capacity

Compression membrane response can only develop if there is a tight fit or very small gap (i.e. less than approximately 0.25 inch) between an infill masonry wall and surrounding rigid frame (e.g. infill walls surrounded by a heavy reinforced concrete frame). The amount of gap for infill walls can vary considerably depending on the construction. The walls can be built "tight" within the surrounding frame or there can be an intentional gap allowing the beam overhead to deflect without bearing on the wall below and for thermal expansion of the wall. Shrinkage of the wall may also increase the gap between the infill wall and surrounding frame. The amount of compression membrane response is very sensitive to the gap dimension and to the quality of the mortar between the masonry units and framing component. The use of compression membrane is not recommended for design due to general uncertainties in the actual gap dimension between the wall and surrounding building framing, the rigidity of the frame, and in quality of the mortar adjacent to the framing.



<!-- batch 31 | page 29 -->

Tension membrane response can develop in reinforced masonry walls at large deflections if there is: 1) fully developed reinforcing bars throughout the span and into the supports; and 2) supports with adequate in-plane rigidity for one-way responding walls. The bars will not remain fully developed throughout the span if composite action between the grout and reinforcing steel deteriorates at high steel strains. Sufficient testing has not been performed to develop specific design and detailing requirements for masonry walls to provide tension membrane resistance during response to blast loads. Also, boundary conditions that allow tension membrane response are not typical in reinforced masonry wall construction. Thus, membrane action may develop in reinforced masonry walls with adequate supports and reinforcing details, but design approaches verified with blast test data are not available.



In summary, tension and compression membrane action should not typically be considered in the design or analysis of masonry walls for explosives safety applications.

### 6 -7.6Dynamic Analysis

The principles for dynamic analysis of structural components subject to blast loads presented in Chapter 3 of this document apply to blast analyses of masonry walls.Therefore, the dynamic response of masonry walls can be modeled using an equivalent SDOF system. The SDOF system consists of a mass-spring system with a mass that is defined by the wall mass and a load-mass factor, and a spring that is defined by a resistance-deflection relationship representing the response of the wall to the blast load (i.e. typically a uniform pressure loading). The wall mass is calculated based on the wall weight and the load-mass factor is determined based on the wall boundary conditions and associated deflected shape of the wall from blast load as discussed in Section 3-7.The resistance-deflection relationship for flexural response is described in Chapter 3.This approach should be used to determine the resistance-deflection relationship for masonry walls. The ultimate dynamic moment capacity and moment of inertia for the wall should be determined as discussed in Sections 6.8 and 6.9 for reinforced and unreinforced masonry walls, respectively. At deflections greater than the yield deflection causing the ultimate flexural resistance, the response of reinforced masonry walls may be considered ductile (i.e. constant resistance equal to the ultimate flexural resistance with increasing deflection), while the response of unreinforced masonry walls is considered britle and the flexural resistance goes to zero. Unreinforced masonry walls develop additional resistance at deflections greater than the yield deflection from the effects of in-plane axial force, including self-weight, which cause arching response described in Section 6-9. Given the one-story construction limitation in Section 6-7.1,the axial compression load on a masonry wall typically is low enough compared to the wall's axial load capacity that the wall's flexural design may ignore combined axial and flexure loads.



The ultimate support shear,$\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{u}}$ , should be calculated at each support for the wall as discussed in Section 3-15. The wall's connections to the supporting frame should be designed to resist the ultimate shear at the support and the wall's shear capacity should 

<!-- batch 31 | page 30 -->

be designed to resist the ultimate shear at the support or at a distance d from the support as discussed in Section 6-7.3.4.



### 6 -7.7Response Criteria

The maximum deflection from the dynamic response is used to calculate a support rotation, as described in Chapter 3, which is compared to the maximum acceptable response criteria for reinforced and unreinforced masonry in Table 6-3 (+) for the required Protection Category, as defined in Chapter 1. The deflection to thickness ratio must also meet the requirements in Table 6-3 for unreinforced masonry. These response criteria are based on ductile flexural reinforced masonry response and brittle unreinforced masonry response with axial load arching, as described in Sections 6.8and 6.9.



<div style="text-align: center;">Table 6-3Response Criteria for Masonry Components </div>



<div style="text-align: center;"><html><body><table border="1"><tr><td rowspan="2">Wall Type</td><td colspan="2">Support Rotation (Degrees)</td><td colspan="2">Deflection to Thickness Ratio</td></tr><tr><td>Protection Category 1</td><td>Protection Category 2</td><td>Protection Category 1</td><td>Protection Category 2</td></tr><tr><td>Steel Reinforced Masonry</td><td>2.0</td><td>6.0</td><td>N/A</td><td>N/A</td></tr><tr><td>Unreinforced Masonry*</td><td>1.5</td><td>4.0</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td colspan="5">Pnly applicable for britle flexural response followed by compression membrane or axial load arching.</td></tr></table></body></html></div>


### 6 -7.8Rebound

After reaching its initial maximum dynamic deflection, a masonry wall typically will deflect back in the opposite direction of the applied blast load (i.e. rebound). This causes a stress reversal in the wall and reaction forces that are in the opposite direction compared to the inbound response. Reinforcing steel located at the center of a masonry wall will resist both inbound and rebound stresses. Otherwise, new masonry walls should be symmetrically reinforced at each face. Unreinforced masonry walls should be analyzed assuming they have no flexural resistance and only axial load arching during rebound unless the inbound displacement was less than the flexural yield deflection, Xe (see Section 6-9.2 and Figure 6-13 (+)). The response limits in Section 6-7.7 also apply during rebound. Masonry wall connections to their supports should resist the ultimate support shear for rebound response.



## 6 -8REINFORCED MASONRY WALLS

### 6 -8.1General Requirements and Construction Procedures

Reinforced masonry walls are designed to resist blast load in a similar manner as reinforced concrete walls, except that the walls must comply with construction requirements and reinforcement steel detailling provisions of TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5.

<!-- batch 31 | page 31 -->

The amount of composite action between the reinforcing steel and masonry is very dependent on the workmanship during wall construction including the grout placement,since the grout transfers the tension strength of the reinforcing steel into the wall. This has been demonstrated in blast test programs on reinforced masonry walls, where walls with careful inspection during construction demonstrated significantly greater blast resistance than walls with no inspection (Oswald, et al 2006). Full composite action is necessary for reinforced masonry to develop a ductile resisting moment based on the dynamic tensile yield strength of reinforcement and the compression strength of the masonry, as assumed for blast design.



All blast resistant reinforced masonry walls should be subject to inspection and material testing during construction as required in Chapter 1 of TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5 for essential facilities (i.e. Level C Quality Assurance). This requirement helps ensure that the reinforcing steel and surrounding grout will be constructed to act compositely with the surrounding masonry before and after yielding of the steel and that there are no voids in solid grouted walls that are intended to provide protection from fragments.Procedures that inhibit full composite action include insufficient vibrating during placement, improper reinforcing steel placement too near an edge of the grouted space,mortar protrusion and droppings into the grouted space, and grout with insufficient strength or plasticity. Additionally, both vertical and horizontal reinforcement should be accurately positioned during wall construction and rigidly secured at intervals by wire ties or spacing devices. The distance between reinforcement and the masonry unit must not be less than -inch for fine grout or /-inch for course grout.

### 6 -8.2Ultimate Flexural Moment Capacity

The ultimate dynamic moment capacity used to calculate the resistance of reinforced masonry walls is shown in Equation 6-9. This equation assumes that reinforced masonry will respond in essentially the same manner to blast loading as reinforced concrete. The applicability of this assumption has been demonstrated in numerous static tests and blast tests on well constructed reinforced masonry walls.

$$\begin{aligned}&M_{u}=\frac{A_{s}f_{dy}}{b}\left(d-\frac{a}{2}\right)\\ &where\quad a=\frac{A_{s}f_{dy}}{0.8bf_{dm}^{*}}\\ \end{aligned}$$

where:

$M_{u}=$ dynamic ultimate moment capacity per unit width 

$A_{s}=$ area of tension reinforcement in width, b 

$\pmb{f}_{d y}=$ dynamic design stress 

$d=$ effective depth (distance from the extreme compression fiber to the centroid of 

the tension reinforcement)



<!-- batch 31 | page 32 -->

b=spacing between reinforcement $f_{d m}^{\prime}=$ masonry prism dynamic compressive strength 

### 6 -8.3Reinforcing Steel Limitations

The maximum bar size for primary vertical reinforcing steel in new construction is a No.6 bar. There is also a limit of one bar per cell at each face of the masonry (i.e. no bundled bars). The purpose for these limitations is to encourage the use of more distributed reinforcement, rather than the use of a smaller number of larger bars. This requirement also helps to limit the demand on the grout to provide composite action between the reinforcement and masonry through large plastic deflections. Dynamic flexural response may be considered for existing walls with larger bar sizes if it can be shown that the masonry and grout can develop the full dynamic yield strength of the bars out to the maximum wall deflection calculated in the dynamic analysis of the wall response to the applied blast load.



Minimum and maximum values for the amount of steel reinforcement for blast design are shown in Table 6-4 (+). The minimum steel ratio for primary steel in the table is based on the requirement in TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5 for beams that the minimum steel ratio should cause a moment capacity that exceeds 1.3 times the masonry cracking moment, assuming that the dynamic modulus of rupture for masonry equals 200 psi and the dynamic reinforcing steel yield strength is 77 ksi. The maximum steel ratio is based on a conservative, historical value from the Unified Building Code of onehalf the balanced steel ratio, assuming a dynamic prism strength of 2380 psi and a dynamic yield strength of 77 ksi for the reinforcing steel. This is less than the maximum steel ratio in TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5, but blast testing shows that more ductility is available in reinforced masonry walls with lower steel ratios (Oswald et al, 2006). The minimum horizontal joint reinforcement is required to provide confinement around splices and distribute loads into the vertical reinforcing steel. Horizontal reinforcement should also be used as required around openings to distribute blast loads into primary vertical flexural steel and to resist conventional loads (e.g. in lintel beams over openings).



The maximum spacing for primary vertical reinforcement is 3 times the nominal wall thickness for partially grouted walls and 6 times the nominal wall thickness for solid or fully grouted walls. The minimum spacing for primary vertical reinforcement in CMU walls is one bar per cell (or one bar at each face within a cell for nominal 12 inch thick CMU walls). Primary vertical reinforcement placed in voids between composite wythes of walls shall have a minimum spacing of 1.5 times the bar diameter, and not less than 1.5 inches. The maximum and minimum spacing for secondary horizontal joint reinforcement is 16 inches and 8 inches, respectively.



<!-- batch 31 | page 33 -->

### 6 -8.4

## Splices and Development of Reinforcement

Reinforcement should have a minimum splice length and development length, Id, as required in TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5, including the material reduction factor (i.e. φfactor). The reinforcing steel and masonry dynamic material strengths should be used to calculate the development length. All splices should be located in regions of low stress (i.e. no more than 75% of the maximum moment) and should be specified on the structural drawings to help ensure that splices are placed as intended. Additionally,splice lengths must be increased 30% if there are bars at each face of a cell (four per cell at splice locations) or if any of the bars are within 3 inches of each other. CMU walls with one layer of spliced reinforcement (i.e. at mid-thickness) should have at least a nominal thickness of 8 inches to help provide sufficient grout in the cells around spliced reinforcing steel. CMU walls with spliced reinforcement at each face should have at least a nominal thickness of 12 inches for the same reason. Non-contact splices are not allowed. Mechanical splices are allowed as permitted in Section 4-21.8.

<div style="text-align: center;">Table 6-4 Minimum and Maximum Steel Ratios for Reinforced Masonry Components </div>



<div style="text-align: center;"><html><body><table border="1"><tr><td>Case</td><td>Reinforcement Area Limitation1 $(\mathbf{A}_{s})$</td></tr><tr><td>Minimum steel ratio for primary steel (vertical steel)</td><td>$A_{s}\geq0.0006bd$</td></tr><tr><td>Maximum steel ratio for primary steel (vertical steel)</td><td>$A_{s}\leq0.005b d$</td></tr><tr><td>Minimum secondary steel (horizontal joint reinforcement)</td><td>Two longitudinal W1.7 wires in bed joints at every other course (16 inches)</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Note 1: See Equation 6-9 for definition of terms</td></tr></table></body></html></div>


Figure 6-10 (+) shows a reinforced CMU wall with spliced reinforcing bars. This figure shows two layers of reinforcement, but a single layer of reinforcement at midthickness is much more typical. Reinforced CMU walls are typically spliced at approximately 4 ft to 5ft height intervals in traditional low-lift wall construction, where the grout is placed by hand in the reinforced cells with a maximum pour height of 5 ft. The need to keep splices out of maximum moment regions may prevent the use of splices at the top of each grout pour and cause larger reinforcing steel lengths and associated block lift heights unless "A" or "H" blocks are used (see Figure 6-1 (+)), which is permitted.

Alternatively, high lift grout placement may be used according to the requirements of TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5, which allows much longer lengths of unspliced reinforcement. The wall is built up to 24 ft high, or higher in some cases, clean-out cells are provided at the bottom of the wall, and grout is placed with a pump from the top of each reinforced cell. In the cases of both low-lift and high-lift construction techniques,inspection and quality assurance are essential to obtain composite action between the steel reinforcement and masonry as assumed for blast design.

<!-- batch 31 | page 34 -->

### 6 -8.5Moment of Inertia

The flexural stiffness of a reinforced masonry component should be based on the average of the cracked moment of inertia and the net, uncracked moment of inertia using Equation 6-10. This is an approximate approach that is not based on measurements of the actual cracking that occurs during dynamic blast response, but is consistent with the approach recommended for reinforced concrete in Section 4-15.This approach has been assumed in previous comparisons where the dynamic deflections calculated in SDOF analyses using Equation 6-10 compared well, on the average, to measured values (Oswald et al, 2006). The importance of the moment of inertia is often reduced by the fact that most wall deflection typically occurs after yielding of the flexural reinforcing steel, when the wall stifness is assumed equal to zero in the SDOF analysis methodology, so that most of the dynamic deflection is not affected by the moment of inertia. The cracked moment of inertia can be calculated using a simplified approach in Equation 6-11, or it can be calculated with the methodology for reinforced concrete in Section 4-15 using $E_{m}$ from Equation 6-2 in place of the modulus of elasticity for concrete (Ec). For solid masonry units, the value of $I_{n}$ is equal to the moment of inertia of the gross section. Values of $I_{n}$ for ungrouted or partially grouted CMU can be determined using information in Section 6-7.2.1.

$$I_{a}=\frac{I_{n}+I_{c}}{2}$$

6-10

where:

$I_{a}=$ the average moment of inertia for the net section and cracked section $(i n^{4}/i n)$ 

$I_{n}=$ the moment of inertia of the net masonry section (in4/in) (see Equation 6-1)

$I_{\tt C}=$ the moment of inertia of the cracked masonry section (in/in) (see Equation 6-11)



$$I_{c}=0.005\;d^{3}$$

where:

$d=$ effective depth (distance from the extreme compression fiber to the centroid of the tension reinforcement)



$b=$ spacing between reinforcement 

## 6 -9

## UNREINFORCED MASONRY WALLS

Unreinforced masonry walls may not be used for new construction. The adequacy of existing unreinforced masonry walls may be evaluated in accordance with Section 6-7.Accordingly, these walls may not be placed in tension and must satisfy the shear (diagonal tension) and direct shear requirements of Sections 6-7.3.4 and 6-7.3.5,

<!-- batch 31 | page 35 -->

respectively. Similarly, the direct spalling, breach, and penetration resistance of fully grouted unreinforced masonry walls may be evaluated in accordance with Sections 67.3.6 and 6-7.3.7.



Per Section 6-7.3.3, existing unreinforced masonry walls may be used to resist farrange blast loading only. Procedures for analyzing the flexural response of these walls are provided in Sections 6-9.1 and 6-9.2. These walls have very limited ductility so the resistance-deflection relationship is based on brittle flexural response. However, these walls have some additional lateral load resistance and a significant amount of additional strain energy (i.e. area under the resistance-deflection curve) after brittle flexural failure from axial load arching caused by self weight and any supported axial load. Accordingly,the resistance-deflection curve for an existing unreinforced CMU wall may include axial load arching, determined in accordance with Section 6-9.2, if the wall is in good condition and laterally supported to resist the ultimate support shear as described in Section 6-7. Axial load arching is caused by applied axial load, and it is therefore different from compression membrane response where in-plane support rigidity causes much higher axial loads to develop in walls. The moment of inertia for unreinforced masonry is equal to the net moment of inertia for the masonry cross section,$I_{n}$ ,which can be determined using information in Section 6-7.2.1.



### 6 -9.1Brittle Flexural Response

Equation 6-12 shows the ultimate dynamic moment capacity of unreinforced masonry walls based on the dynamic modulus of rupture strength of the wall. Values for the elastic section modulus can be determined for CMU as discussed in Section 6-7.2.1.The dynamic flexural tension strength of masonry should be calculated as discussed in Section 6-7.3.3. The dynamic moment capacity is used to determine the ultimate flexural resistance of the wall. The flexural stiffness is calculated using the gross, or net moment of inertia as discussed in Section 6-7.2.1.



$$\boldsymbol{M}_{d u}=\boldsymbol{S}\left(\boldsymbol{f}_{d t}+\frac{\boldsymbol{P}}{\boldsymbol{A}}\right)$$

where:

$M_{d u}=$ ultimate dynamic moment capacity $\pmb{f}_{d t}=$ dynamic modulus of rupture strength of masonry wall $S=$ elastic section modulus of masonry unit $P=$ compressive load per unit width along wall $A=$ cross sectional area per unit width along wall 

<!-- batch 31 | page 36 -->

### 6 -9.2Axial Load Arching

For the general case where there is no compression membrane response, the resistance of an unreinforced masonry wall will reducesuddenlyimmediatelyafterthe wall resistance reaches the ultimate flexural resistance due to the brittle nature of the tensile bond between the mortar and masonry in the maximum moment regions. The wall will then develop axial load arching, which is characterized by a much lower resistance than flexural response, and a uch largerdeflection to failure.Thisprovides a significant increase in the strain energy and blast load capacity of unreinforced masonry walls, although the wall is still a weak component against blast load. An example of the resistance-deflection curve for an unreinforced masonry wall with britle flexural response followed by axial load arching is shown in Figure 6-11 (+). Note that the axial load is an applied load, and is therefore different from compression membrane response that depends on the in-plane rigidity of the supports to develop axial load. The applied axial load should only include the supported dead load.

Figure 6-12 (+) illustrates the mechanics of axial load arching. As significant rotation occurs at midspan and at the supports after britle flexural failure, the axial load is resisted at the unloaded face or the wall near the supports and at the loaded face near midspan. The resulting in—plane axial force couple (i.e. axial load arching) resists lateral load. The peak magnitude of the resisting couple, equal to $\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{a}}$ in Equation 6-13, is a function of the applied axial load, including component self-weight above midspan, and the component thickness. Note that the axial load from self weight in Equation 6-13 is assumed to act along the same line of action as the axial load, P, in Figure 6-12 when,in fact, it acts along a line of action through the center of gravity of the wall. This is a simplification that is supported by comparisons of SDOF analysis using Equation 6-13to measured response of unreinforced masonry walls with axial load from self-weight (Oswald, 2005). The ultimate resistance and stiffness for flexural response used to calculate $\mathsf{X_{e}}$ in Equation 6-13 are calculated using methods in Chapter 3. Non-load bearing walls have axial load arching due only to axial load from their self-weight.

$$r_{a}=\frac{8}{L^{2}}(T-2X_{e})\left(P+\frac{wL}{2}\right)\quad where\quad X_{e}=\frac{r_{u}}{K_{e}}$$

where:

$r_{a}{=}$ maximum resistance from axial load effects 

$X_{e}=$ deflection at ultimate flexural resistance 

$T=$ overall wall thickness 

$P=$ compressive axial load width along wall 

$w=$ areal self-weight and supported weight of wall 

$L=$ span length equal to wall height 

$r_{u}=$ ultimate flexural resistance of wall (see Section 6-9.1)

$K_{e}=$ elastic unit stiffness of wall (or equivalent elastic stiffness for walls with 



<!-- batch 31 | page 37 -->

## indeterminate supports)

Figure 6-13 (+) shows the resistance-deflection relationship for unreinforced masonry with brittle flexural response and axial load arching. All resistance is due to flexural response until flexural yielding has occurred at all maximum moment regions and the component becomes a mechanism (i.e. at a deflection $\mathsf{X}_{\mathtt{e}}$ in Figure6-13).At deflections between $\tt{2X_{e}}$ and the component thickness, h, in Figure 6-13 (+),all resistance is due to axial load arching.The resistance-deflection relationship transitions between these two responses modes in Figure 6-13 (i.e. between $\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{e}}$ and $2\dot{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathsf{e}})$ at an assumed slope slighttly less than the elastic stiffness. The load-mass factor is based on elastic flexural response, as discussed in Section 3-7, for the wall response up to the deflection at ultimate flexural resistance, Xe.The load-mass factor is based on plastic flexural response, as discussed in Section 3-7,for the response at all larger deflections, where the wall has cracked at the maximum moment region(s), and is assumed to have the same deflected shape it has after yielding at all maximum moment regions.or twoway spanning walls, axial load arching acts only in the vertical spa 

### 6 -9.3Unreinforced Multi-Wythe Walls

Multi-wythe walls are generally designed to deflect together in a non-composite manner for conventional loads using ties to transfer load from the outer wythe to the inner wythe, as discussed in Section 6-7.3.For existing unreinforced masonry multi-wythe walls where it can be shown that the tie anchors will allow the outer wall to transfer load to the inner wall without failing according to the requirements in Section 6-7.3, the wall system can be analyzed as multiple walls responding together in flexure in a noncomposite manner analogous to a system of springs in parallel arrangement responding to a load. All the walls deflect equally, causing the system of walls to have a total flexural stiffness equal to the summed stiffnesses of all walls and to resist the applied blast load in proportion to their relative flexural stiffnesses. This is consistent with the approach recommended in TMS 402/ACI 530/ASCE 5.



The walls are assumed to resist lateral load in this manner until the wall with the highest stresses yields in tension at all maximum moment locations to become a mechanism.The combined flexural resistance of all the walls when this occurs is the ultimate brittle flexural resistance of the multi-wythe wall system. Each wall must resist its ultimate support shear based on its maximum resistance.



After peak flexural response, the multi-wythe wall system can have axial load arching.The peak resistance from axial load arching is calculated separately for each wall and summed to determine the total peak axial load arching resistance for the wall system (i.e.$\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{a}}$ in Figure 6-13 (+)). The axial load arching resistance is assumed to decrease linearly to zero resistance at a deflection equal to the average wythe thickness, or to the thickness of the wythe providing the largest axial load arching resistance, whichever is less.



<!-- batch 31 | page 38 -->

The response of a multi-wythe wall system .3with adequate connectors between wythes analyzed in this manner will only increase significantly if the two wythe thicknesses are nearly equal. For example, if the ratio of wythe thicknesses in a two-wythe wall system is equal to 2.0, the value of ultimate flexural resistance of the two-wythe system will only be 1.125 times greater than that for thicker wythe. This contrasts sharply with the case of a system with two equal wythe thicknesses, where the ultimate flexural resistance of the wall system is 2.0 times greater than the resistance of either wythe./1/
